<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds in a simple group of type <i>F</i><sub>∞</sub>. This resolves the higher-finiteness strengthening of the Boone–Higman conjecture.

- **claim-main-embedding** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): Every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem admits an injective homomorphism into a nontrivial simple group $H$ of type $F_\infty$.
  - Explanation: Every finitely generated group with a decidable word problem sits inside a nontrivial simple group having a classifying space with finitely many cells in each dimension.
- **claim-idempotent-annihilation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-idempotents)): Let $T$ be an abelian group, and suppose that elements $z_E\in T$, indexed by $E\in\mathcal P_\ell$, satisfy $$\label{eq:idempotent-additivity} z_{E+P}=z_E+z_P$$ whenever $E,P,E+P\in\mathcal P_\ell$ and $E,P$ are orthogonal. Then $\ell z_I=0$.
  - Explanation: An additive assignment on the finite orthogonal idempotent system forces the class of the identity to be killed by the characteristic.
- **claim-ascending-torus-finiteness** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-torus)): Let $f:U\to U$ be an injective endomorphism. Assume the following.* 1. *There are a finitely presented group $P$ and homomorphisms $a:U\to P$, $b:P\to U$ with $f=ba$.* 2. *For each $\ell\in\{2,3\}$ there are homomorphisms $h_E:U\to U$, indexed by $E\in\mathcal P_\ell$, such that $h_I=f$. Whenever $E,P',E+P'\in\mathcal P_\ell$ and $EP'=P'E=0$, the subgroups $h_E(U)$ and $h_{P'}(U)$ commute elementwise, and $$h_{E+P'}(u)=h_E(u)h_{P'}(u)\qquad(u\in U).$$* *Then $T(U,f)$ is of type $F_\infty$.
  - Explanation: An ascending torus of an injective endomorphism has type F-infinity when the endomorphism factors through a finitely presented group and two commuting idempotent-indexed systems of splittings exist.
- **claim-torus-finite-presentation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-torus-presentation)): Under assumption <span class="upright">(i)</span> of Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>, the group $T(U,f)$ is finitely presented. This assertion does not require $f$ to be injective.
  - Explanation: Factoring the defining endomorphism through a finitely presented group already gives a finite presentation of the torus, without injectivity.
- **claim-product-module-criterion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-product-criterion)): Suppose that $J$ is finitely presented and that $$H_n\bigl(J,(\mathbb ZJ)^{\mathcal I}\bigr)=0 \qquad(n\ge2)$$ for every set $\mathcal I$. Then $J$ is of type $F_\infty$.
  - Explanation: For a finitely presented group, vanishing of homology with coefficients in arbitrary products of the group ring in high degrees gives type F-infinity.
- **claim-controlled-additivity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-controlled-additivity)): Fix $n\ge1$ and a bound $A$. Suppose that a power $f^k$ induces a chain map $F:C_*^A(U)\to C_*(U)$ admitting a controlled chain homotopy to the constant map at $1$ through degrees less than $n$. Let $h_1,h_2:U\to U$ have elementwise commuting images, and put $h(u)=h_1(u)h_2(u)$. There is a finite bound $B$ such that every $n$-cycle $z\in C_n^A(U)$ satisfies $$[(h f^k)_*z]=[(h_1f^k)_*z]+[(h_2f^k)_*z] \quad\text{in }H_n(C_*^B(U)).$$ The same output bound may be chosen for any fixed finite collection of such pairs of maps.
  - Explanation: For commuting maps, the induced action on cycle classes is additive inside one finite output bound once lower degrees are controllably contracted.
- **claim-uniform-fillings** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-uniform-fillings)): Under the assumptions of Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>, for every finite bound $A$ and every $n\ge0$ there are an integer $k\ge0$ and a finite bound $B$ such that $f^k$ sends every reduced integral $n$-cycle of $C_*^A(U)$ to a boundary in $C_*^B(U)$.
  - Explanation: A single iterate and a single finite bound fill every bounded reduced cycle, uniformly over all cycles.
- **claim-finite-index-extension** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-finite-extension)): If $K$ is a normal subgroup of finite index in $L$ and $K$ has type $F_\infty$, then $L$ has type $F_\infty$.
  - Explanation: Type F-infinity passes from a finite-index normal subgroup to the whole group.
- **claim-enlargement-homomorphisms** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-enlargement)): Each $j_E$ is a homomorphism. The map $j_I$ is injective, and together with $z\mapsto\mathbf e_z$ it embeds the acting pair. For every indicated orthogonal sum $E+P$ in $\mathcal P_\ell$, the images of $j_E$ and $j_P$ commute elementwise and $$j_{E+P}(g)=j_E(g)j_P(g).$$ If $gz=z$, then $j_E(g)\mathbf e_z=\mathbf e_z$ for every $E$.
  - Explanation: The enlargement maps are homomorphisms, embed the acting pair, satisfy the orthogonal product identities and preserve fixed points.
- **claim-ring-data** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-ring-data)): For every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem, there is a nonzero finitely presented unital $\mathbb F_2$-algebra $B$ with the following properties.* 1. *There is an injective nonunital algebra endomorphism $\phi:B\to B$. With $p=\phi(1)$ there are $s_0,s_1,d_0,d_1\in pBp$, centralizing $\phi(B)$, such that $$\label{eq:ring-binary-pairs} d_i s_j=\delta_{ij}p,\qquad s_0d_0+s_1d_1=p.$$* 2. *Form $Q,X,W,Y,\iota,\iota_X$ and $j_{\ell,E}$ as above. There is a nonzero idempotent $e\in B$ such that $G$ and $W$ embed in $(eBe)^\times$. There are $S_y,T_y\in eBe$, for all $y\in Y$, with $$\label{eq:ring-prefix-data} T_yS_z=\delta_{yz}e,\qquad wS_y=S_{wy},\qquad T_yw=T_{w^{-1}y}.$$ Here $w$ denotes its corner-unit image.* *The two diagrams have common full-identity map $\iota$ and satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:diagram-fixed-points" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagram-fixed-points">[eq:diagram-fixed-points]</a>.
  - Explanation: Every such group embeds in a finitely presented algebra over the two-element field carrying a nonunital endomorphism with centralizing binary corner data and prefix data for the enlarged action.
- **claim-bounded-compiler** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-compiler)): Fix a sufficiently large finite alphabet $\Sigma$ containing the distinguished letters of the compiler. From an index $\eta$ for a partial function $f_\eta:\Sigma^*\rightharpoonup\Sigma^*$ one can compute a finite monoid presentation $K_\eta$, with alphabet $\mathcal A_\eta$ and zero letter $\Omega$, and an additive nonnegative integral word grade $\gamma:\mathcal A_\eta^*\to\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$. Letter grades are at most two, and $\gamma(\Omega)=0$.* *There is a partial algorithm $N_\eta:\mathcal A_\eta^*\rightharpoonup\mathcal A_\eta^*$, independent of any budget, with the following property. Suppose that, for some $m\geq0$, $$\begin{aligned} &f_\eta(u)\text{ is defined and }\|f_\eta(u)\|\leq \|u\| &&(\|u\|\leq m),\label{eq:compiler-budget-total}\\ &f_\eta(a f_\eta(u)b)=f_\eta(aub) &&(\|aub\|\leq m).\label{eq:compiler-budget-context} \end{aligned}$$ Then $N_\eta$ terminates through grade $m$. Each such input is connected to its literal output by a defining-equation derivation that never raises grade. A contextual defining-equation step preserves the output if both endpoints have grade at most $m$. Consequently, $$\label{eq:compiler-context} N_\eta(u)=N_\eta(v)\ \Longrightarrow\ N_\eta(aub)=N_\eta(avb) \quad\text{if }\gamma(aub),\gamma(avb)\leq m.$$ Unconditionally, $N_\eta$ fixes the empty word and $\Omega$, sends every word containing $\Omega$ to $\Omega$, and fixes a specified literal word $\operatorname{raw}(u)$ for every $u\in\Sigma^*$. The raw words are mutually distinct and distinct from the empty word and $\Omega$. If the hypotheses hold at every $m$, then $N_\eta$ decides equality in $K_\eta$ and $$\label{eq:compiler-raw-product} N_\eta\bigl(\operatorname{raw}(u)\operatorname{raw}(v)\bigr) =\operatorname{raw}\bigl(f_\eta(uv)\bigr).$$ The presentation is computed without evaluating $f_\eta$. Apart from finite computations, $N_\eta$ calls $f_\eta$ only on inputs of length at most the grade of its own input.
  - Explanation: From an index of a partial function one computes a graded monoid presentation whose normalizer decides equality through a grade whenever the stated budget hypotheses hold.
- **claim-action-monoid-normal-form** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-action-monoid-normal-form)): Every nonzero element of $M(V,Z)$ has a unique normal form $$\mathsf s_{z_1}\cdots\mathsf s_{z_a}\,g\, \mathsf t_{w_1}\cdots\mathsf t_{w_b}, \qquad a,b\geq0,$$ omitting an identity group label. In particular, $V$ embeds as a group of units.
  - Explanation: The action monoid has unique normal forms, so the acting group embeds as units.
- **claim-bounded-action-calculus** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-bounded-action-calculus)): Suppose the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:compiler" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compiler">11</a> hold through length $3d$, where $d\geq0$. Within any one of the three families, evaluation of any symbol sequence of cost at most $d$, and equality comparison of two such sequences (each separately of cost at most $d$), terminate using compiler calls of grade at most $3d$. Tested equality respects replacing a subword in a product whenever both whole symbol sequences have cost at most $d$. Tests agree at different valid budgets.
  - Explanation: Under the compiler hypotheses through a bounded length, evaluation and equality tests of bounded cost terminate and respect substitution.
- **claim-normalizer-total** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-encoding-bootstrap)): The function $f_\eta$ is total and length-nonincreasing. Its rule tables are stable on bounded-length words and confluent on those words. In particular, $$\label{eq:encoding-context-law} f_\eta(a f_\eta(u)b)=f_\eta(aub) \qquad(a,u,b\in\Sigma^*).$$
  - Explanation: The self-referential normalizer is total, never lengthens words, and is compatible with contexts, proved by induction on length.
- **claim-encoding-embeddings** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-encoding-embeddings)): The code families embed $G$, $T_\eta$, and $M(W,Y)$ unitally into $L$, preserving zeros in the last two cases. There are injective algebra maps $$\label{eq:encoding-algebra-maps} \kappa:B\otimes_k k[\mathcal D]\longrightarrow A_*, \qquad i:A_*\longrightarrow B,$$ where $\kappa$ is unital and $i$ has a nonzero proper support identity $e=i(1)$.
  - Explanation: The code families embed the needed groups and monoids, and two injective algebra maps link the algebras, one with a proper support identity.
- **claim-ring-finite-presentation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-encoding-finite-presentation)): The algebra $B$ is nonzero and finitely presented as a unital associative $\mathbb F_2$-algebra, and as a unital ring.
  - Explanation: The constructed algebra is nonzero and finitely presented both as an algebra and as a ring.
- **claim-highly-transitive-action** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-action)): For every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem, there is a group $\Lambda$ containing $G$ and acting faithfully on a countably infinite set $S$ such that:* 1. *the action is transitive on ordered tuples of distinct points of every finite length;* 2. *$\Lambda$ and every pointwise stabilizer of a finite tuple are of type $F_\infty$.
  - Explanation: The group embeds in a group with a faithful highly transitive action on a countable set in which the group and all finite-tuple pointwise stabilizers have type F-infinity.
- **claim-compressed-diagonal-elementary** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-diagonal)): For every $r\ge2$ and $v\in B^\times$, $$D_1\bigl(1+\phi(v-1)\bigr)\in\mathrm E_r(B).$$
  - Explanation: A compressed single-place diagonal unit lies in the elementary matrix group.
- **claim-finite-permutations-realised** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-stage-permutations)): Every permutation of $\sigma(X)$ with finite support is induced on that set by an element of $Q$.
  - Explanation: Every finitely supported permutation of the compressed column set is induced by an element of the elementary group.
- **claim-faithful-high-transitivity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-stage-faithful-transitive)): The set $S$ is countably infinite. The action of $\Lambda$ on $S$ is faithful and transitive on ordered tuples of distinct points of every finite length.
  - Explanation: The limit set is countably infinite and the limit group acts faithfully and transitively on distinct tuples of every finite length.
- **claim-stabilizer-is-torus** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-torus)): The pointwise stabilizer $\Lambda_{([z,0])}$ is the ascending torus of $U$ under $f$.
  - Explanation: The pointwise stabilizer of the chosen point is an ascending torus of the stabilizer subgroup under the induced endomorphism.
- **claim-kernel-annihilation** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-stage-kernel)): Let $R$ be a unital associative ring and let $\psi:R\to R$ be an additive multiplicative map. Put $P=\psi(1)$. Suppose that $U_0,U_1,V_0,V_1\in PRP$ commute with $\psi(R)$ and satisfy $$V_iU_j=\delta_{ij}P, \qquad U_0V_0+U_1V_1=P.$$ Then the homomorphism $$\theta:\mathrm{St}_6(R)\longrightarrow\mathrm{St}_6(R), \qquad x_{ij}(a)\longmapsto x_{ij}(\psi(a)),$$ annihilates the kernel of $\pi$.
  - Explanation: An endomorphism built from centralizing binary corner data induces a Steinberg map killing the kernel of the projection to the elementary group.
- **claim-steinberg-lift** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-steinberg-lift)): There is a homomorphism $L:B^\times\to\mathrm{St}_6(B)$ such that $$\label{eq:stage-lift} \pi L(v)=D_1\bigl(1+\phi^2(v-1)\bigr).$$ Moreover, $\mathrm{St}_6(B)$ is finitely presented.
  - Explanation: Units lift homomorphically to a finitely presented Steinberg group with the prescribed matrix image.
- **claim-stabilizer-factorization** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-factor)): The endomorphism $f$ in Equation <a href="#eq:stage-stabilizer-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:stage-stabilizer-map">[eq:stage-stabilizer-map]</a> factors through $\mathrm{St}_6(B)$ by homomorphisms $$U\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathrm{St}_6(B)\xrightarrow{b}U.$$
  - Explanation: The stabilizer endomorphism factors through the Steinberg group by homomorphisms, with the second factor landing in the stabilizer.
- **claim-twisted-transfer** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-twisted-transfer)): Let $A$ act faithfully and oligomorphically on a countably infinite set $S$. Suppose $A$ and the setwise stabilizer of every finite subset of $S$ have type $F_\infty$. Then $SV_A$ is a nontrivial simple group of type $F_\infty$ and contains $A$ through its global coordinate action.
  - Explanation: A faithful oligomorphic action whose group and finite-set stabilizers have type F-infinity yields a nontrivial simple group of type F-infinity containing the acting group.

        "anchor": "stmt-thm-ring-data",
        "exact_quote": "For every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem, there is a nonzero finitely presented unital $\\mathbb F_2$-algebra $B$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n1.  *There is an injective nonunital algebra endomorphism $\\phi:B\\to B$. With $p=\\phi(1)$ there are $s_0,s_1,d_0,d_1\\in pBp$, centralizing $\\phi(B)$, such that $$\\label{eq:ring-binary-pairs}\n       d_i s_j=\\delta_{ij}p,\\qquad s_0d_0+s_1d_1=p.$$*\n\n\n2.  *Form $Q,X,W,Y,\\iota,\\iota_X$ and $j_{\\ell,E}$ as above. There is a nonzero idempotent $e\\in B$ such that $G$ and $W$ embed in $(eBe)^\\times$. There are $S_y,T_y\\in eBe$, for all $y\\in Y$, with $$\\label{eq:ring-prefix-data}\n       T_yS_z=\\delta_{yz}e,\\qquad\n       wS_y=S_{wy},\\qquad T_yw=T_{w^{-1}y}.$$ Here $w$ denotes its corner-unit image.*\n\n*The two diagrams have common full-identity map $\\iota$ and satisfy Equation <a href=\"#eq:diagram-fixed-points\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:diagram-fixed-points\">[eq:diagram-fixed-points]</a>.",
        "source_id": "PM-250.2"
      },

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-thm-ring-data",
          "exact_quote": "For every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem, there is a nonzero finitely presented unital $\\mathbb F_2$-algebra $B$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n1.  *There is an injective nonunital algebra endomorphism $\\phi:B\\to B$. With $p=\\phi(1)$ there are $s_0,s_1,d_0,d_1\\in pBp$, centralizing $\\phi(B)$, such that $$\\label{eq:ring-binary-pairs}\n       d_i s_j=\\delta_{ij}p,\\qquad s_0d_0+s_1d_1=p.$$*\n\n\n2.  *Form $Q,X,W,Y,\\iota,\\iota_X$ and $j_{\\ell,E}$ as above. There is a nonzero idempotent $e\\in B$ such that $G$ and $W$ embed in $(eBe)^\\times$. There are $S_y,T_y\\in eBe$, for all $y\\in Y$, with $$\\label{eq:ring-prefix-data}\n       T_yS_z=\\delta_{yz}e,\\qquad\n       wS_y=S_{wy},\\qquad T_yw=T_{w^{-1}y}.$$ Here $w$ denotes its corner-unit image.*\n\n*The two diagrams have common full-identity map $\\iota$ and satisfy Equation <a href=\"#eq:diagram-fixed-points\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:diagram-fixed-points\">[eq:diagram-fixed-points]</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-250.2"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem, there is a nonzero finitely presented unital $\\mathbb F_2$-algebra $B$ with the following properties.*\n\n\n1.  *There is an injective nonunital algebra endomorphism $\\phi:B\\to B$. With $p=\\phi(1)$ there are $s_0,s_1,d_0,d_1\\in pBp$, centralizing $\\phi(B)$, such that $$\\label{eq:ring-binary-pairs}\n       d_i s_j=\\delta_{ij}p,\\qquad s_0d_0+s_1d_1=p.$$*\n\n\n2.  *Form $Q,X,W,Y,\\iota,\\iota_X$ and $j_{\\ell,E}$ as above. There is a nonzero idempotent $e\\in B$ such that $G$ and $W$ embed in $(eBe)^\\times$. There are $S_y,T_y\\in eBe$, for all $y\\in Y$, with $$\\label{eq:ring-prefix-data}\n       T_yS_z=\\delta_{yz}e,\\qquad\n       wS_y=S_{wy},\\qquad T_yw=T_{w^{-1}y}.$$ Here $w$ denotes its corner-unit image.*\n\n*The two diagrams have common full-identity map $\\iota$ and satisfy Equation <a href=\"#eq:diagram-fixed-points\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:diagram-fixed-points\">[eq:diagram-fixed-points]</a>.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-compiler",
        "exact_quote": "Fix a sufficiently large finite alphabet $\\Sigma$ containing the distinguished letters of the compiler. From an index $\\eta$ for a partial function $f_\\eta:\\Sigma^*\\rightharpoonup\\Sigma^*$ one can compute a finite monoid presentation $K_\\eta$, with alphabet $\\mathcal A_\\eta$ and zero letter $\\Omega$, and an additive nonnegative integral word grade $\\gamma:\\mathcal A_\\eta^*\\to\\mathbb Z_{\\geq0}$. Letter grades are at most two, and $\\gamma(\\Omega)=0$.*\n\n\n\n*There is a partial algorithm $N_\\eta:\\mathcal A_\\eta^*\\rightharpoonup\\mathcal A_\\eta^*$, independent of any budget, with the following property. Suppose that, for some $m\\geq0$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n &f_\\eta(u)\\text{ is defined and }|f_\\eta(u)|\\leq |u|\n       &&(|u|\\leq m),\\label{eq:compiler-budget-total}\\\\\n &f_\\eta(a f_\\eta(u)b)=f_\\eta(aub)\n       &&(|aub|\\leq m).\\label{eq:compiler-budget-context}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Then $N_\\eta$ terminates through grade $m$. Each such input is connected to its literal output by a defining-equation derivation that never raises grade. A contextual defining-equation step preserves the output if both endpoints have grade at most $m$. Consequently, $$\\label{eq:compiler-context}\n N_\\eta(u)=N_\\eta(v)\\ \\Longrightarrow\\\n N_\\eta(aub)=N_\\eta(avb)\n \\quad\\text{if }\\gamma(aub),\\gamma(avb)\\leq m.$$ Unconditionally, $N_\\eta$ fixes the empty word and $\\Omega$, sends every word containing $\\Omega$ to $\\Omega$, and fixes a specified literal word $\\operatorname{raw}(u)$ for every $u\\in\\Sigma^*$. The raw words are mutually distinct and distinct from the empty word and $\\Omega$. If the hypotheses hold at every $m$, then $N_\\eta$ decides equality in $K_\\eta$ and $$\\label{eq:compiler-raw-product}\n N_\\eta\\bigl(\\operatorname{raw}(u)\\operatorname{raw}(v)\\bigr)\n   =\\operatorname{raw}\\bigl(f_\\eta(uv)\\bigr).$$ The presentation is computed without evaluating $f_\\eta$. Apart from finite computations, $N_\\eta$ calls $f_\\eta$ only on inputs of length at most the grade of its own input.",
        "source_id": "PM-250.2"
      },

The raw words are mutually distinct and distinct from the empty word and $\\Omega$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-bounded-action-calculus",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose the hypotheses of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compiler\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compiler\">11</a> hold through length $3d$, where $d\\geq0$. Within any one of the three families, evaluation of any symbol sequence of cost at most $d$, and equality comparison of two such sequences (each separately of cost at most $d$), terminate using compiler calls of grade at most $3d$. Tested equality respects replacing a subword in a product whenever both whole symbol sequences have cost at most $d$. Tests agree at different valid budgets.",
        "source_id": "PM-250.2"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **decidable word problem** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A finitely generated group has *decidable word problem* if an algorithm answers this question for every word.
- **type F_n and type F_infinity** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A group $H$ has *type $F_n$* if it admits a classifying space $K(H,1)$ with finite $n$-skeleton, and has *type $F_\infty$* if it admits such a space with finitely many cells in each dimension.
- **oligomorphic action** ([source](#pm-h-3)): The action must be *oligomorphic*, meaning that it has finitely many orbits on finite subsets of each fixed cardinality.
- **highly transitive action** ([source](#pm-h-3)): We construct a *highly transitive* action: any two ordered tuples of distinct points of the same finite length lie in the same orbit.
- **ascending torus** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For a group $U$ and an endomorphism $f:U\to U$, write $$T(U,f)=\langle U,t\mid t^{-1}ut=f(u)\ (u\in U)\rangle.$$
- **prefix algebra D_ell** ([source](#pm-h-7)): For $\ell\in\{2,3\}$, let $D_\ell$ be the unital $\mathbb F_\ell$-algebra of operators generated by $l_0,l_1,r_0,r_1$ on the free vector space with basis $\{0,1\}^{\mathbb N}$.
- **bound** ([source](#pm-h-10)): We call $A$ a *bound*.
- **contracted monoid algebra** ([source](#pm-h-17)): For a field $k$ and a monoid $M$ with absorbing zero, write $k_0[M]$ for its *contracted monoid algebra*: its basis consists of the nonzero elements of $M$, and a monoid product equal to zero is interpreted as algebra zero.
- **cost** ([source](#pm-h-17)): Their *cost* is the maximum grade of a monomial, with cost zero for the zero expression.
- **shortlex order** ([source](#pm-h-17)): The *shortlex* order compares words first by length and then lexicographically.
- **brick** ([source](#sec:simple)): A *brick* in $\mathcal C^S$ prescribes a finite binary prefix $\alpha(s)$ in each coordinate $s$, with all but finitely many prefixes empty.
- **twisted Brin–Thompson group** ([source](#sec:simple)): The *twisted Brin–Thompson group* $SV_A$ consists of the homeomorphisms represented by two finite brick partitions and a bijection between their pieces, with each piece map of the form $$h_\beta\tau_a h_\alpha^{-1}:B_\alpha\longrightarrow B_\beta.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **r-idempotents** [claim](#stmt-lem-idempotents) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-idempotents): Work in rank-three matrix algebras, show multiples of the diagonal units agree, then use the split into two orthogonal idempotents. -> the characteristic times the identity class vanishes
- **r-torus-presentation** [claim](#stmt-lem-torus-presentation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-torus-presentation): Interleave the two tori by mutually inverse homomorphisms. -> finite presentation of the torus
- **r-product-criterion** [claim](#stmt-lem-product-criterion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-product-criterion): Inductively attach cells, using vanishing with product coefficients to make the cycle module finitely generated. -> classifying space with finitely many cells in each dimension
- **r-controlled-additivity** [claim](#stmt-lem-controlled-additivity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-controlled-additivity): Bounded diagonal and shuffle chain maps with a controlled homotopy. -> additivity of cycle classes in a bounded complex
- **r-uniform-fillings** [claim](#stmt-prop-uniform-fillings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-uniform-fillings): Induction on degree building the controlled homotopy, then apply additivity to the idempotent relations. -> uniform iterate and bound filling all bounded cycles
- **r-torus-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-torus) / [proof](#pm-h-11): Show the coefficient shift is locally nilpotent on the tree sequence, so homology with product coefficients vanishes. -> the torus has type F-infinity
- **r-finite-extension** [claim](#stmt-lem-finite-extension) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-finite-extension): Build a degreewise finite free resolution using product vanishing for the subgroup. -> type F-infinity for the finite-index overgroup
- **r-enlargement** [claim](#stmt-lem-enlargement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-enlargement): Direct multiplication using orthogonality of the summands. -> homomorphisms, embedding and fixed-point property
- **r-compiler-cited** [claim](#stmt-lem-compiler) / [proof](#pm-h-15): Imported from the companion article. -> graded monoid presentation with a budget-independent normalizer
- **r-normal-form** [claim](#stmt-lem-action-monoid-normal-form) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-action-monoid-normal-form): Orient the relations and check overlaps for confluence. -> unique normal forms
- **r-bounded-calculus** [claim](#stmt-lem-bounded-action-calculus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-bounded-action-calculus): Truncated algebra, matrix and action operations, then confluence on abstract labeled words with minimum costs. -> bounded evaluation and substitution-compatible equality
- **r-bootstrap** [claim](#stmt-lem-encoding-bootstrap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-encoding-bootstrap): Fixed point by Kleene recursion, then simultaneous induction on length using the strict dependency of bounds, termination and Newman's lemma. -> total, length-nonincreasing, context-compatible normalizer
- **r-embeddings** [claim](#stmt-lem-encoding-embeddings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-encoding-embeddings): Raw-word map and subtraction of the zero image. -> injective algebra maps with a proper support identity
- **r-finite-presentation** [claim](#stmt-lem-encoding-finite-presentation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-encoding-finite-presentation): Binomial relations on the finite monoid presentation. -> finite presentation of the algebra
- **r-ring-data** [claim](#stmt-thm-ring-data) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-encoding-finite-presentation-2): Transport the code embeddings and the external binary corner through the algebra maps. -> ring with endomorphism, binary corners and prefix data
- **r-diagonal** [claim](#stmt-lem-diagonal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-diagonal): Abelian quotient of the diagonal-extended elementary group and orthogonal equivalent idempotents. -> compressed diagonal is elementary
- **r-permutations** [claim](#stmt-lem-stage-permutations) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stage-permutations): Explicit unit built from prefix data, then compressed diagonal. -> elements of Q realising finite permutations
- **r-faithful-transitive** [claim](#stmt-lem-stage-faithful-transitive) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stage-faithful-transitive): Direct-limit stages are strictly increasing, so powers of the shift are not in N; transitivity from finite permutations. -> faithful highly transitive action
- **r-stabilizer-torus** [claim](#stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-torus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-torus): Height homomorphism and semidirect product decomposition. -> stabilizer as ascending torus
- **r-kernel-cited** [claim](#stmt-thm-stage-kernel) / [proof](#eq:stage-nonunital-matrix): Imported from the companion article. -> kernel of the projection is annihilated
- **r-steinberg-lift** [claim](#stmt-lem-steinberg-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-steinberg-lift): Apply kernel annihilation to descend to a lift, and cite finite presentability of Steinberg groups. -> homomorphic lift into a finitely presented group
- **r-stabilizer-factor** [claim](#stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-factor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-factor): Signed exchange supported on an idempotent fixing the marked columns, composed with the lift. -> factorization through the Steinberg group
- **r-action-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-action) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-stage-stabilizer-factor-2): Combine transitivity, torus identification, commuting splittings and the factorization with the ascending-torus theorem. -> action with finite-type stabilizers
- **r-transfer-cited** [claim](#stmt-thm-twisted-transfer) / [proof](#sec:simple): Imported from the work of Belk and Zaremsky. -> simple overgroup of type F-infinity
- **r-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-twisted-transfer): Pass from pointwise to setwise stabilizers by finite extension and apply the transfer theorem. -> embedding into a nontrivial simple group of type F-infinity

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-embedding-simple-finf**: A finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds injectively in a nontrivial simple group of type F_infinity.
- **inv-ascending-torus-finf**: An ascending torus of an injective endomorphism that factors through a finitely presented group and carries two commuting idempotent-indexed families of endomorphisms is of type F_infinity.
- **inv-product-module-criterion**: A finitely presented group whose integral homology with coefficients in arbitrary products of the regular module vanishes from degree two on is of type F_infinity.
- **inv-finite-index-extension**: Type F_infinity passes from a normal subgroup of finite index to the ambient group.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-complexity-transfer**: Expect a bounded-time word-problem algorithm to follow from the embedding into the simple overgroup. -> The input decider may be arbitrarily slow and the embedding carries no complexity guarantee.
- **anti-finite-dimension**: Assume the classifying space of the simple overgroup has finite dimension or the group is torsion-free. -> Type F_infinity only gives finitely many cells in each dimension.
- **anti-linear-oligomorphic**: Feed an oligomorphic action of a linear group or a group of finite virtual cohomological dimension into the twisted Brin–Thompson construction. -> Some finite-set stabilizer then fails type F_infinity, so the transfer theorem does not apply.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main results assert existence of infinite groups, actions and classifying spaces of type F_infinity, obtained through Kleene's recursion theorem and a length induction over an arbitrary decidable word problem; no finite procedure with a checkable input and output is stated, so there is nothing to execute and test.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The main theorem is existential and relies on a Kleene fixed point and unbounded searches over a countable alphabet. No finite procedure emerges that a program could execute as a library. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns simulation, dynamics or game mechanics. The groups and actions are infinite abstract objects. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No explicit tool-ready algorithm is given. The only parallel search in the paper is the classical decision argument for finitely presented simple groups, and it is used here only for the converse. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The compiler and the normalizer are partial and self-referential, and their totality is proved only by induction on length. There is no complexity bound and no terminating algorithm output. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The action monoid normal forms and the rule tables are proof devices. The paper gives no storage layout or operation costs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper relates to parallel hardware or circuits. The matrix constructions are over infinite abstract rings. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardness assumptions, protocols or security claims appear. Decidability of word problems is a qualitative property, not a cryptographic primitive. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning, optimisation or statistical objects occur. The results are purely algebraic and homological. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no objective functions, constraints or search for optima. The shortlex least representative is a proof device. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No communication, consensus or replicated-state model is present. The only parallel search is a classical dovetailing argument in the converse. |
| other | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The paper serves as a reference for foundational mathematical tooling: certifying that a candidate group embeds in a simple group of type F_infinity, and for formal verification of the finiteness criteria. Only an adaptable use, with no constructive code. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-decidable-word-problem** (hypothesis): The main embedding needs the group to be finitely generated with a decidable word problem. The converse shows these hypotheses cannot be dropped, since finitely generated subgroups of finitely presented simple groups inherit a decidable word problem. Blocked use: ['Applying the embedding to a group with undecidable word problem', 'Applying the embedding to an infinitely generated group']
- **lim-no-complexity-transfer** (edge_case): The input word-problem algorithm may have arbitrary running time, so no complexity bound is transferred to the overgroup. Blocked use: ['Expecting a polynomial-time word-problem algorithm in the simple overgroup', 'Quoting a running time for the construction']
- **lim-finite-type-only** (formal_scope): Type F_infinity gives finite skeleta in every dimension only. It does not give torsion-freeness or finite cohomological dimension. Blocked use: ['Assuming the classifying space of the overgroup is finite dimensional', 'Assuming the overgroup is torsion-free']
- **lim-torus-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The ascending-torus criterion requires injectivity of the endomorphism, a factorization through a finitely presented group, and both commuting idempotent-indexed systems. Finite presentation alone follows from the factorization only. Blocked use: ['Concluding type F_infinity from the factorization alone', 'Applying the criterion to a non-injective endomorphism']
- **lim-product-coefficients** (formal_scope): The product-module criterion uses direct products, not direct sums, of the regular module, and vanishing is required for every index set, in degrees from two on. Blocked use: ['Checking vanishing only for direct-sum coefficients', 'Checking vanishing only for one index set']
- **lim-compiler-budget** (hypothesis): The compiler guarantees termination and context compatibility only through the grade where its budget hypotheses hold. Totality at all lengths comes from the length induction. Blocked use: ['Using the normalizer beyond the grade where the hypotheses are established', 'Assuming termination before the induction is done']
- **lim-nonconstructive-fixed-point** (nonconstructive): The program index comes from Kleene's recursion theorem and termination is not assumed in advance. The construction is not an effective procedure with stated resource bounds. Blocked use: ['Presenting the construction as a runnable algorithm with a complexity bound']
- **lim-stabilizer-obstruction** (counterexample_scope): Oligomorphic actions of groups of finite virtual cohomological dimension or countable linear groups always have a finite-set stabilizer failing type F_infinity, so the transfer cannot use them. The construction builds group and stabilizers together. Blocked use: ['Feeding an action of a linear group into the twisted Brin–Thompson construction']
- **lim-transfer-hypotheses** (hypothesis): The twisted transfer needs a faithful oligomorphic action on a countably infinite set, with the group and every finite-subset setwise stabilizer of type F_infinity. Pointwise stabilizers pass to setwise ones by the finite-index lemma. Blocked use: ['Applying the transfer to a non-faithful action', 'Applying it without checking setwise stabilizers']
- **lim-finite-index-normal** (hypothesis): Passage of type F_infinity to an overgroup requires a normal subgroup of finite index. Blocked use: ['Using it for infinite-index or non-normal subgroups']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 250 (`lean/docs/250.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Finite algebraic envelopes and the Boone–Higman conjecture](../../preprints/Finite-algebraic-envelopes-and-the-Boone-Higman-conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Simple $F_\infty$ overgroups of groups with decidable word problem](../../preprints/Simple-F-infinity-overgroups-of-groups-with-decidable-word-problem-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A universal group of type $F_\infty$](../../preprints/A-universal-group-of-type-F-infinity-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The Boone–Higman conjecture characterizes finitely generated groups with decidable word problem by embeddings into finitely presented simple groups. The formalization proves the equivalence in full: a finitely generated group has a computable word problem for a finite generating set exactly when it embeds by an injective homomorphism into a finitely presented simple group.
>
> The formalization proves the higher-finiteness strengthening of the Boone–Higman conjecture: every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds by an injective homomorphism into a nontrivial simple group of type $F_\infty$. Here type $F_\infty$ means that the group has a classifying CW complex with finitely many cells in each dimension. No additional finiteness property of the original group is assumed.
>
> The formalized result constructs one group $H$ of type $F_\infty$ containing every finitely presented group. Here type $F_\infty$ means that $H$ has a classifying space with finitely many cells in each dimension.
>
> The group $H$ is fixed before the groups embedded into it are chosen. No word-problem assumption is imposed, and the classifying space need not be finite-dimensional or have finitely many cells in total. The paper's converse about recursively presented subgroups is not included.

| declaration | kind |
|---|---|
| `evalWord` | def |
| `HasDecidableWordProblem` | def |
| `HasTypeFInfty` | def |
| `main` | theorem |

Comparator source `SimpleOvergroups.lean` (1354 bytes, sha256 `72d81cb8600211d955645ef46129f11b4c348bc0b9d18078270ab67e9511bb80`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace SimpleFInftyOvergroups
universe u

def evalWord {G : Type u} [Group G] {n : ℕ} (g : Fin n → G)
    (w : List (Fin n × Bool)) : G :=
  (w.map fun x => if x.2 then g x.1 else (g x.1)⁻¹).prod

def HasDecidableWordProblem (G : Type u) [Group G] : Prop :=
  ∃ (n : ℕ) (g : Fin n → G), Subgroup.closure (Set.range g) = ⊤ ∧
    ComputablePred (fun w : List (Fin n × Bool) => evalWord g w = 1)

def HasTypeFInfty (H : Type u) [Group H] : Prop :=
  ∃ (X : Type u) (topology : TopologicalSpace X),
    letI : TopologicalSpace X := topology
    ∃ (cw : Topology.CWComplex (Set.univ : Set X)),
      letI : Topology.CWComplex (Set.univ : Set X) := cw
      T2Space X ∧ ConnectedSpace X ∧
      Topology.CWComplex.FiniteType (Set.univ : Set X) ∧
      ∃ (x : X), Nonempty (H ≃* FundamentalGroup X x) ∧
        ∃ (E : Type u) (coverTopology : TopologicalSpace E),
          letI : TopologicalSpace E := coverTopology
          ∃ (p : E → X), IsCoveringMap p ∧ Function.Surjective p ∧ ContractibleSpace E

theorem main (G : Type u) [Group G] [Group.FG G]
    (hG : HasDecidableWordProblem G) :
    ∃ (H : Type u) (_ : Group H), Nontrivial H ∧ IsSimpleGroup H ∧
      HasTypeFInfty H ∧ ∃ f : G →* H, Function.Injective f := by
  sorry

end SimpleFInftyOvergroups

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Every finitely generated group with decidable word problem embeds in a simple group of type <i>F</i><sub>∞</sub>. This resolves the higher-finiteness strengthening of the Boone–Higman conjecture.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The word problem asks whether a word in a fixed finite generating set and its inverses represents the identity. A finitely generated group has *decidable word problem* if an algorithm answers this question for every word. Embedding theorems connect this algorithmic condition with the algebraic structure of an overgroup. Higman’s embedding theorem characterizes the finitely generated subgroups of finitely presented groups as the recursively presented groups: those admitting a recursively enumerable set of defining relations (Higman 1961) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Higman1961](#ref-Higman1961)]. Boone and Higman obtained the finer characterization (Boone and Higman 1974, Theorem I) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)]: a finitely generated group has decidable word problem if and only if it embeds in a simple group which in turn embeds in a finitely presented group. They asked whether the simple overgroup itself can always be finitely presented (Boone and Higman 1974, 43) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)]. This is the Boone–Higman conjecture. Thompson subsequently showed that the intermediate simple group can be chosen finitely generated with decidable word problem (Thompson 1980) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Tho80](#ref-Tho80)].

We consider the strengthening that asks for finiteness in every homotopical dimension. A group $H$ has *type $F_n$* if it admits a classifying space $K(H,1)$ with finite $n$-skeleton, and has *type $F_\infty$* if it admits such a space with finitely many cells in each dimension. Here a classifying space is a connected CW complex with fundamental group $H$ and contractible universal cover. Type $F_1$ is finite generation and type $F_2$ is finite presentation. The question of a simple overgroup of type $F_\infty$ appears in the discussion following Question 5.6 of Belk et al. (2025) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[BBMZsurvey](#ref-BBMZsurvey)]; Question 5.6 itself asks about overgroups of type $F_\infty$ for arbitrary finitely presented groups.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Every finitely generated group $G$ with decidable word problem admits an injective homomorphism into a nontrivial simple group $H$ of type $F_\infty$.*

</div>

The input need not be finitely presented, and its word-problem algorithm may have arbitrary running time. The classifying space of $H$ may have infinitely many dimensions; type $F_\infty$ imposes neither torsion-freeness nor finite cohomological dimension. The theorem answers the higher-finiteness question positively and in particular implies the Boone–Higman conjecture.

The classical converse gives an algebraic characterization here as well (Boone and Higman 1974) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BH74](#ref-BH74)]. A nontrivial finitely presented simple group has decidable word problem: in parallel, enumerate consequences of its defining relations and consequences after adding the input word as a relation. A trivial input word is detected by the first search; a nontrivial one normally generates the simple group, so the second search eventually proves all generators trivial. These outcomes are exclusive because the original group is nontrivial. Restricting this algorithm along an embedding solves the word problem in any finitely generated subgroup. Thus the groups in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> are exactly the finitely generated subgroups of simple groups of type $F_\infty$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Earlier embeddings and the higher-finiteness obstacle

Thompson-type groups have long supplied simple groups with strong finiteness properties. Brown established type $F_\infty$ for the Thompson–Higman families (Brown 1987, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Brown1987](#ref-Brown1987)]. More recently, Belk and Zaremsky constructed a single simple group of type $F_\infty$ containing all finitely generated right-angled Artin groups (Belk and Zaremsky 2022, Corollary E) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BelkZaremsky2022](#ref-BelkZaremsky2022)]; these are the groups presented by generators indexed by the vertices of a finite graph, with commutation relations for its edges. Belk, Hyde and Matucci constructed a simple group of type $F_\infty$ containing every countable abelian group (Belk, Hyde, and Matucci 2024, Theorems 4.4–4.6) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BHMgerms](#ref-BHMgerms)]. Such results give substantial classes of examples without a general construction from a word-problem algorithm.

Other recent advances concern the finite-presentation conclusion of the Boone–Higman conjecture. Belk, Bleak, Matucci and Zaremsky proved it for hyperbolic groups and contracting self-similar groups (Belk, Bleak, et al. 2026, Theorem A and Corollary D) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BBMZhyperbolic](#ref-BBMZhyperbolic)]. Belk, Fournier-Facio, Hyde and Zaremsky proved it for $\operatorname{Aut}(F_n)$ and mapping class groups of orientable punctured surfaces of finite type (Belk, Fournier-Facio, et al. 2026, Theorem A and Corollary B) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BFFHZ](#ref-BFFHZ)]. Here $F_n$ denotes the free group of rank $n$. These conclusions do not by themselves give simple overgroups of type $F_\infty$. Higher finiteness requires control of additional relations among relations in every dimension.

Our final step uses the twisted Brin–Thompson construction of Belk and Zaremsky (2022) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BelkZaremsky2022](#ref-BelkZaremsky2022)]. Its input is a faithful action of a group $\Lambda$ on a countably infinite set $S$. The action must be *oligomorphic*, meaning that it has finitely many orbits on finite subsets of each fixed cardinality. If $\Lambda$ and all its finite-set stabilizers have type $F_\infty$, their theorem gives a simple group $SV_\Lambda$ of type $F_\infty$ containing $\Lambda$. Consequently the problem is to embed $G$ in a group equipped with such an action, including the finiteness properties of its stabilizers. We construct a *highly transitive* action: any two ordered tuples of distinct points of the same finite length lie in the same orbit. This supplies the orbit condition. We prove type $F_\infty$ for the pointwise stabilizers; the corresponding setwise stabilizers are finite extensions and inherit the property.

The stabilizer requirement is a genuine restriction on the choice of acting group. Fournier-Facio, Kropholler, Lyman and Zaremsky prove that an oligomorphic action on an infinite set of a group of finite virtual cohomological dimension, or of a countable group linear over a field, always has a finite-set stabilizer which is not of type $F_\infty$ (Fournier-Facio et al. 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[FFKLZstabilizers](#ref-FFKLZstabilizers)]. The construction below therefore builds the overgroup and its stabilizers together; it does not ask the original group to possess an action with these properties.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Constructing the action and proving finiteness

The action begins with a finitely presented ring $B$, the elementary matrix group $Q=\mathrm E_7(B)$, and its action on the column set $X=B^7$. Here $\mathrm E_r(B)$ is generated by the off-diagonal elementary matrices. We construct compatible injections $$\delta:Q\longrightarrow Q,\qquad \sigma:X\longrightarrow X.$$ At each new stage, matrices can permute any finite collection of the compressed columns $\sigma(X)$. The direct limits of these two systems, with the stage shift adjoined to the group, give the action $\Lambda\curvearrowright S$. This makes high transitivity a local matrix calculation. The same construction embeds $G$ into $Q$.

The ring has to encode more than the given group. Its units must also encode prescribed enlargements of the action of $\mathrm E_7(B)$ on $B^7$, so the objects being encoded already involve the ring being constructed. We resolve this self-reference by a length induction. A finite compiler from the companion article *Finite algebraic envelopes and the Boone–Higman conjecture* (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[FoundationsCompanion](#ref-FoundationsCompanion)] gives equality calculations at each finite bound when its stated hypotheses hold. The encoding ensures that every recursive call uses a strictly smaller bound. Kleene’s recursion theorem first supplies the program, and the length induction then proves its totality and compatibility with multiplication. The companion also supplies a binary corner construction and a Steinberg-kernel annihilation theorem; their exact statements appear where they are used.

Finiteness is supplied by a separate ascending-torus criterion. For an injective endomorphism $f:U\to U$, write $$T(U,f)=\langle U,t\mid t^{-1}ut=f(u)\ \text{for }u\in U\rangle.$$ A factorization of $f$ through a finitely presented group gives a finite presentation of $T(U,f)$. To obtain higher finiteness, we use two finite systems of homomorphisms whose images commute in specified pairs. Their product identities come from orthogonal idempotents over $\mathbb F_2$ and $\mathbb F_3$. After lower-dimensional terms have been killed, commuting products act additively on homology. The two systems then force the same class to be annihilated by both $2$ and $3$.

The essential point is uniformity. We use simplices whose vertices are elements of $U$, and regard two simplices as having the same type when they differ by a simultaneous left translation. One iterate and one finite set of output types work for every cycle using a given finite set of input types. Chain lengths and coefficients may be arbitrarily large. This control allows fillings with coefficients in products of copies of the group ring, and the Bieri–Eckmann finiteness method then gives finite skeleta in every dimension. The result is Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>.

The acting group and each finite-point stabilizer are themselves ascending tori to which this same criterion applies. For stabilizers, the defining endomorphism factors through a finitely presented Steinberg group. The second factor sends the *entire* Steinberg group into the stabilizer, not merely the image of the first factor. The commuting diagrams preserve the fixed points as well. These two properties establish type $F_\infty$ for all stabilizers and complete the input to the twisted Brin–Thompson theorem.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization and conventions

Section <a href="#sec:homological" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:homological">2</a> proves the ascending-torus criterion. Section <a href="#sec:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:encoding">3</a> constructs the ring and the encoded actions. Section <a href="#sec:stage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:stage">4</a> constructs $\Lambda\curvearrowright S$ and proves the stabilizer theorem. Section <a href="#sec:simple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:simple">5</a> applies the twisted Brin–Thompson theorem and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

All rings used for matrices are unital and associative. Ring embeddings and endomorphisms need not preserve the identity when this is expressly stated. All homology is integral. Group actions are on the left; coefficient modules for group homology are right modules.

<a id="sec:homological"></a>
### A homological device for ascending tori

An ascending torus will have type $F_\infty$ for two distinct reasons. Factoring its defining endomorphism through a finitely presented group provides finite presentation. Two finite systems of commuting splittings then give the higher-dimensional finiteness: one system annihilates the relevant homology classes by $2$, and the other annihilates the same classes by $3$. The factorization also starts this second argument, by placing the image of the endomorphism in a finitely generated subgroup.

We first construct the finite systems of idempotents that index the splittings. The resulting criterion is Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>. Its proof must control a finite set of simplex types uniformly over all cycles; it will impose no bound on chain lengths or coefficients.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Two finite systems of idempotents

For $\ell\in\{2,3\}$, let $D_\ell$ be the unital $\mathbb F_\ell$-algebra of operators generated by $l_0,l_1,r_0,r_1$ on the free vector space with basis $\{0,1\}^{\mathbb N}$. The operator $l_i$ prefixes $i$, whereas $r_i$ deletes an initial $i$ and sends every other basis vector to zero. Thus <a id="eq:binary-prefix-relations"></a>$$\label{eq:binary-prefix-relations}
 r_i l_j=\delta_{ij}1,
 \qquad l_0r_0+l_1r_1=1.$$ For a finite binary word $v$, write $l_v$ for prefixing $v$, write $r_v$ for deleting $v$, and put $E_v=l_vr_v$.

Every product of these operators is zero or a prefix replacement $l_vr_w$. Equality of finite linear combinations of prefix replacements is decidable. Indeed, choose $m$ at least as large as every domain prefix length and expand each term by $$l_vr_w=\sum_{|a|=m-|w|}l_{va}r_{wa}.$$ Collect terms with the same domain prefix and the same output prefix. For a fixed domain prefix, distinct output prefixes give distinct outputs on an input whose remaining tail is not eventually periodic. To see this, equality of two such outputs with different prefixes would make the shorter prefix a prefix of the longer and would force the remaining tail to be periodic. The collected coefficients therefore all vanish if and only if the operator vanishes. This is a finite test over $\mathbb F_\ell$.

Put $$A=E_{00},\quad B'=E_{01},\quad C'=E_1,
 \qquad A_0=E_{000},\quad A_1=E_{001}.$$ For each of the three prefix-free lists $$(00,01,1),\qquad (000,01,1),\qquad (001,01,1),$$ the operators $l_vr_w$ form matrix units for a copy of $M_3(\mathbb F_\ell)$ in $D_\ell$. Only the first of these copies has identity $1$. Let $\mathcal P_\ell$ be the union of their sets of idempotents, together with $I=1$. This is a finite, explicitly specified set. In the following statement, orthogonal means $EP=PE=0$.

<a id="stmt-lem-idempotents"></a>
<div id="lem:idempotents" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:idempotent-additivity"></a>
**Lemma 2**. *Let $T$ be an abelian group, and suppose that elements $z_E\in T$, indexed by $E\in\mathcal P_\ell$, satisfy $$\label{eq:idempotent-additivity}
 z_{E+P}=z_E+z_P$$ whenever $E,P,E+P\in\mathcal P_\ell$ and $E,P$ are orthogonal. Then $\ell z_I=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-idempotents"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-idempotents</summary>

*Proof.* Within a rank-three matrix algebra, we will show that the $\ell$-multiples attached to its diagonal units agree. The overlapping corners then identify these multiples for $A,A_0,A_1,B',C'$, and the split $A=A_0+A_1$ forces their common value to vanish.

First work in any one of the three matrix algebras. For a column vector $u$ and a row covector $\lambda$ with $\lambda u=1$, write $x(u,\lambda)=z_{u\lambda}$. Fix $v\ne0$ in $\ker\lambda$. We claim that $$x(u+v,\lambda)-x(u,\lambda)$$ is independent of the choice of $u$ with $\lambda u=1$.

Suppose first that $u'-u$ and $v$ are linearly independent. The vectors $u,u'-u,v$ then form a basis, so there is a covector $\mu$ with $$\mu u=\mu u'=0,\qquad \mu v=1.$$ The identity $$u\lambda+v\mu=(u+v)\lambda+v(\mu-\lambda)$$ expresses the same rank-two idempotent in two ways as a sum of orthogonal rank-one idempotents. Applying <a href="#eq:idempotent-additivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:idempotent-additivity">[eq:idempotent-additivity]</a> gives <a id="eq:rank-one-difference"></a>$$\label{eq:rank-one-difference}
 x(u+v,\lambda)-x(u,\lambda)
   =x(v,\mu)-x(v,\mu-\lambda).$$ The same calculation for $u'$ gives the same difference. If instead $u'-u$ belongs to the span of $v$, choose $w\in\ker\lambda\setminus\langle v\rangle$ and set $u''=u+w$. Both $u''-u$ and $u''-u'$ are independent of $v$, so comparison through $u''$ proves the claim. This argument also applies over $\mathbb F_2$.

Apply the claim successively at $u,u+v,\ldots,u+(\ell-1)v$. The resulting differences are equal, and their sum is zero. Hence $$\ell x(u+v,\lambda)=\ell x(u,\lambda).$$ Transposing the argument shows that the $\ell$-multiple is also unchanged when the covector is varied with the vector fixed and its value still equal to one. For two different basis vectors $e_i,e_j$, take $\lambda=e_i^*+e_j^*$. Change the covector at $e_i$, change the vector from $e_i$ to $e_j$, and then change the covector at $e_j$. It follows that the $\ell$-multiples of all three diagonal units agree.

The overlaps among our three matrix algebras now give $$\ell z_A=\ell z_{B'}=\ell z_{C'}
            =\ell z_{A_0}=\ell z_{A_1}.$$ Call their common value $q$. Since $A=A_0+A_1$, relation <a href="#eq:idempotent-additivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:idempotent-additivity">[eq:idempotent-additivity]</a> gives $q=2q$, and therefore $q=0$. Finally, $I=(A+B')+C'$, with $A+B'$ an idempotent in the first matrix algebra. Two applications of the same relation yield $\ell z_I=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The ascending-torus theorem

For a group $U$ and an endomorphism $f:U\to U$, write $$T(U,f)=\langle U,t\mid t^{-1}ut=f(u)\ (u\in U)\rangle.$$ Here the notation includes all relations already holding in $U$.

<a id="stmt-thm-torus"></a>
<div id="thm:torus" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3**. *Let $f:U\to U$ be an injective endomorphism. Assume the following.*

1.  *There are a finitely presented group $P$ and homomorphisms $a:U\to P$, $b:P\to U$ with $f=ba$.*

2.  *For each $\ell\in\{2,3\}$ there are homomorphisms $h_E:U\to U$, indexed by $E\in\mathcal P_\ell$, such that $h_I=f$. Whenever $E,P',E+P'\in\mathcal P_\ell$ and $EP'=P'E=0$, the subgroups $h_E(U)$ and $h_{P'}(U)$ commute elementwise, and $$h_{E+P'}(u)=h_E(u)h_{P'}(u)\qquad(u\in U).$$*

*Then $T(U,f)$ is of type $F_\infty$.*

</div>

We first establish finite presentation. A product-module criterion then reduces the remaining assertion to a precise homology-vanishing statement. Uniformly controlled fillings will supply that vanishing through the homology sequence of the ascending torus.

<a id="stmt-lem-torus-presentation"></a>
<div id="lem:torus-presentation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *Under assumption <span class="upright">(i)</span> of Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>, the group $T(U,f)$ is finitely presented. This assertion does not require $f$ to be injective.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-torus-presentation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-torus-presentation</summary>

*Proof.* Set $g=ab:P\to P$, and let $s$ be the stable letter of $T(P,g)$. There is a homomorphism $$T(U,f)\longrightarrow T(P,g),\qquad u\longmapsto a(u),\quad
 t\longmapsto s.$$ An inverse sends $p\in P$ to $t b(p)t^{-1}$ and sends $s$ to $t$. The defining conjugation relations verify that these are homomorphisms; their composites are the identity because $t f(u)t^{-1}=u$ and $s g(p)s^{-1}=p$. This is the algebraic interleaving of the two direct systems. No injectivity of $g$ is needed. Finally, a finite presentation of $P$, together with $s$ and its conjugation relations on a finite generating set of $P$, is a finite presentation of $T(P,g)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### A product-module criterion

The product-module test below is a form of the Bieri–Eckmann finiteness criterion (Bieri and Eckmann 1974, Proposition 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BieriEckmann1974](#ref-BieriEckmann1974)]. For the cellular passage from homological to geometric finiteness, see also Brown (Brown 1975, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Brown1975](#ref-Brown1975)]. We give the argument for the now finitely presented group $J=T(U,f)$: vanishing with products of regular modules will yield finite skeleta of a classifying space.

All group homology below has integral coefficients, and coefficient modules are right modules. For a group $J$ and a set $\mathcal I$, the notation $(\mathbb ZJ)^{\mathcal I}$ means the direct product, not the direct sum, with its coordinatewise right action.

<a id="stmt-lem-product-criterion"></a>
<div id="lem:product-criterion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *Suppose that $J$ is finitely presented and that $$H_n\bigl(J,(\mathbb ZJ)^{\mathcal I}\bigr)=0
 \qquad(n\ge2)$$ for every set $\mathcal I$. Then $J$ is of type $F_\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-product-criterion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-product-criterion</summary>

*Proof.* Start with a finite presentation complex and its simply connected universal cover. Inductively, suppose we have a connected CW complex with fundamental group $J$, finite $n$-skeleton, and $(n-1)$-connected universal cover, where $n\ge2$. Its universal-cover $n$-skeleton will be denoted by $Z$. Attach cells of dimensions at least $n+1$, without a finiteness requirement, to obtain a contractible free $J$-complex $\widetilde Z$. This can be done successively by killing homotopy groups. Its augmented cellular chain complex is a free resolution over $R=\mathbb ZJ$, and its modules $C_i$ are finitely generated for $i\le n$.

Let $\mathcal I$ be the set of all cellular $n$-cycles in $Z$, and put $M=R^{\mathcal I}$. For every finite-rank free left $R$-module $C$, the coordinate map gives an isomorphism <a id="eq:finite-rank-product-tensor"></a>$$\label{eq:finite-rank-product-tensor}
 M\otimes_R C\ \longrightarrow\ C^{\mathcal I},
 \qquad (m_i)_{i\in\mathcal I}\otimes c
             \longmapsto (m_i c)_{i\in\mathcal I}.$$ Using this in degrees $n$ and $n-1$, the family whose coordinate indexed by a cycle $z$ is $z$ itself defines a cycle in $M\otimes_R C_n$. By hypothesis, it is the boundary of some $y\in M\otimes_R C_{n+1}$.

Choose an $R$-basis for $C_{n+1}$. Although that basis can be infinite, the tensor $y$ uses only finitely many of its elements, say $c_1,\ldots,c_s$. Taking coordinates of its boundary equation shows that every $n$-cycle is an $R$-linear combination of $\partial c_1,\ldots,\partial c_s$. Thus the $R$-module of $n$-cycles in $Z$ is finitely generated. This argument does not assume that $C_{n+1}$ has finite rank.

Since $Z$ is $(n-1)$-connected, the natural Hurewicz isomorphism (Hatcher 2002, Theorem 4.37) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Hatcher2002](#ref-Hatcher2002)] identifies $\pi_n(Z)$ with $H_n(Z;\mathbb Z)$. There are no $(n+1)$-cells in $Z$, so the latter group is precisely its group of cellular $n$-cycles. Choose spheres representing the finite list of module generators and attach their $J$-translates. Downstairs, this attaches finitely many $(n+1)$-cells; upstairs, it produces an $n$-connected universal cover. Induction gives a complex with finitely many cells in each dimension whose universal cover is weakly contractible. That cover is a CW complex, so it is contractible by Whitehead’s theorem (Hatcher 2002, Theorem 4.5) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Hatcher2002](#ref-Hatcher2002)]. The resulting complex is the required $K(J,1)$. ◻

</details>

For $J=T(U,f)$, finite presentation is already available. It therefore remains to prove $$H_n\bigl(J,(\mathbb ZJ)^{\mathcal I}\bigr)=0
 \qquad(n\ge2)$$ for every set $\mathcal I$. A tensor chain with these coefficients may have arbitrarily large supports in its separate coordinates. The next argument retains the finite set of simplex types shared by all coordinates; this is the control needed to fill such chains.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Uniformly bounded simplex types

Let $\mathcal E(U)$ be the simplicial set whose $q$-simplices are tuples $(u_0,\ldots,u_q)$ of elements of $U$. Faces delete entries and degeneracies repeat entries. Its normalized integral chain complex is denoted by $C_*(U)$. Thus tuples with consecutive equal entries are zero, and the boundary is the alternating sum of faces. The augmentation sends each vertex to $1$. Simultaneous left translation gives a free $U$-action. Its augmented chain complex is contractible as a complex of abelian groups: prepending the identity gives a contraction. Consequently its augmented chain complex is a free resolution of the trivial left $\mathbb ZU$-module.

For a finite symmetric set $A\subseteq U$ containing $1$, let $\mathcal E_A(U)$ consist of the tuples satisfying $$u_i^{-1}u_j\in A\qquad\text{for all }i,j,$$ and write $C_*^A(U)$ for its normalized chains. This is a simplicial subset. We call $A$ a *bound*. A chain operator is controlled on a given input bound if all its output chains lie in $C_*^{A'}(U)$ for some finite output bound $A'$ in each of the degrees under consideration. There is no restriction on the number of terms in an individual output chain.

For each fixed degree $q$, $C_q^A(U)$ has only finitely many basis elements up to left translation: translate the first vertex to $1$, after which every other vertex belongs to $A$. We refer to these orbits as simplex types. Every homomorphism of groups sends a finite bound to a finite bound. Unions of finitely many bounds may always be replaced by one finite symmetric bound containing $1$.

Coning at $1$ fills every reduced cycle in the full complex $C_*(U)$. It does not, however, preserve a common finite bound: on a simplex with vertex $u$, it introduces the difference $u$ itself. This becomes an obstruction when the same bound must work for all translates and all cycles of $C_*^A(U)$. We instead construct fillings after an iterate of $f$, using bounds independent of the individual cycle.

The first step is a controlled additivity calculation in integral chain complexes. Its partial homotopy need not be $U$-equivariant. Later we assemble these ordinary fillings into fillings with product coefficients. We will not tensor the chosen homotopy over $\mathbb ZU$.

<a id="stmt-lem-controlled-additivity"></a>
<div id="lem:controlled-additivity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6**. *Fix $n\ge1$ and a bound $A$. Suppose that a power $f^k$ induces a chain map $F:C_*^A(U)\to C_*(U)$ admitting a controlled chain homotopy to the constant map at $1$ through degrees less than $n$. Let $h_1,h_2:U\to U$ have elementwise commuting images, and put $h(u)=h_1(u)h_2(u)$. There is a finite bound $B$ such that every $n$-cycle $z\in C_n^A(U)$ satisfies $$[(h f^k)_*z]=[(h_1f^k)_*z]+[(h_2f^k)_*z]
       \quad\text{in }H_n(C_*^B(U)).$$ The same output bound may be chosen for any fixed finite collection of such pairs of maps.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-controlled-additivity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-controlled-additivity</summary>

*Proof.* Let $H_q$ be the given homotopy operators for $0\le q<n$, and set $H_q=0$ in all other nonnegative degrees. The chain map $$F'=F-\partial H-H\partial$$ is constant at the identity in degree zero and zero in degrees $1,\ldots,n-1$. Since $H_n=0$, it satisfies $F'z=Fz$ for every $n$-cycle $z$. Choose a common bound for $F$, $F'$, and the finitely many operators $H_q$ used here.

<a id="eq:aw-diagonal"></a>
The diagonal argument below eliminates mixed product terms using lower-degree vanishing. The same mechanism appears in the diagonal-and-Künneth induction for mitotic groups (Baumslag, Dyer, and Heller 1980, Proposition 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[BDH1980](#ref-BDH1980)]. Here we need an integral chain calculation retaining a common finite bound. We use the classical Alexander–Whitney and shuffle comparison of product and tensor chain complexes (Eilenberg and Zilber 1953) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[EilenbergZilber1953](#ref-EilenbergZilber1953)], recording the formulas and their support control. Define the diagonal tensor-chain map by $$\label{eq:aw-diagonal}
 D(u_0,\ldots,u_q)
  =\sum_{i=0}^q
       (u_0,\ldots,u_i)\otimes(u_i,\ldots,u_q).$$ The tensor differential has the convention $\partial(c\otimes d)=\partial c\otimes d+
(-1)^{\deg c}c\otimes\partial d$. The terms at the two sides of each interior cut cancel, showing that $D$ is a chain map. Formula <a href="#eq:aw-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:aw-diagonal">[eq:aw-diagonal]</a> descends to normalized chains.

The shuffle map $S$ sends a tensor $(u_0,\ldots,u_p)\otimes(v_0,\ldots,v_q)$ to the signed sum of the chains in $U\times U$ obtained by following all monotone lattice paths from $(0,0)$ to $(p,q)$ and replacing a lattice point $(i,j)$ by $(u_i,v_j)$. Its sign is $(-1)^N$, where $N$ counts pairs in which a vertical step precedes a horizontal step. Deleting a corner of a path cancels with the path traversing that corner in the other order; the remaining faces are exactly the tensor differential. Hence $S$ is a chain map, also on normalized chains. Both $D$ and $S$ preserve the bound in each coordinate.

Let $\Delta$ be the chain map induced by $u\mapsto(u,u)$. There is a controlled chain homotopy between $\Delta$ and $SD$. Here is a direct construction. For a basis simplex $c$ with vertex set $V$, both maps are supported on the full tuple complex on $V\times V$. Construct a homotopy $R$ inductively on degree. In degree zero the two maps agree, so take $R_0=0$. Having defined it on faces, the chain $$\Delta c-SDc-R\partial c$$ is a cycle supported on that same tuple complex. It bounds there by prepending the vertex $(u_0,u_0)$, where $u_0$ is the first vertex of $c$. Choose this cone for $R(c)$ and extend on the normalized basis. Every coordinate vertex used is a vertex of $c$, so $R$ preserves the bound in both coordinates. This proves the needed homotopy without any assumption about chain lengths.

Write $f^k\times f^k:U\times U\to U\times U$ for the product homomorphism. On the input complex, $$\Delta F=(f^k\times f^k)_*\Delta
 \simeq (f^k\times f^k)_*SD=S(F\otimes F)D.$$ The homotopy is $(f^k\times f^k)_*R$ from the preceding construction; the last equality is naturality of the shuffle. Thus it is controlled on the input bound. Next, the usual signed tensor operator $$H\otimes F+F'\otimes H$$ is a homotopy between $F\otimes F$ and $F'\otimes F'$. In the second summand the value on $c\otimes d$ includes the sign $(-1)^{\deg c}$. Indeed its homotopy boundary is $$(F-F')\otimes F+F'\otimes(F-F')
       =F\otimes F-F'\otimes F'.$$ All chains involved have finite bounds separately in the two coordinates, because this calculation uses only the already controlled operators.

For an $n$-cycle $z$, applying $F'\otimes F'$ to $Dz$ leaves only bidegrees $(0,n)$ and $(n,0)$. The result is $$(1)\otimes F'z+F'z\otimes(1)
     =(1)\otimes Fz+Fz\otimes(1).$$ Shuffling a vertex against a chain merely holds that coordinate fixed. Finally apply the homomorphism $$U\times U\longrightarrow U,
 \qquad (u,v)\longmapsto h_1(u)h_2(v).$$ It is a homomorphism because the two images commute elementwise. For coordinate bounds $B_1,B_2$, a difference in its image belongs to the finite set $h_1(B_1)h_2(B_2)$. Thus all preceding homotopies remain controlled after this map. Their two surviving terms give the asserted identity. Taking a union of output bounds proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-uniform-fillings"></a>
<div id="prop:uniform-fillings" class="proposition">

**Proposition 7**. *Under the assumptions of Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>, for every finite bound $A$ and every $n\ge0$ there are an integer $k\ge0$ and a finite bound $B$ such that $f^k$ sends every reduced integral $n$-cycle of $C_*^A(U)$ to a boundary in $C_*^B(U)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-uniform-fillings"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-uniform-fillings</summary>

*Proof.* The word “reduced” matters only in degree zero. The subgroup $b(P)$ is finitely generated and contains $f(U)$. Choose a finite symmetric generating set for $b(P)$. Every vertex $f(u)$ can be joined to $1$ by a finite edge path whose differences belong to that set. Consequently every reduced zero-cycle is filled after applying $f$, with a bound independent of the cycle. This proves the base case. Notice that the paths need not have uniformly bounded lengths.

Suppose that the proposition is known in degrees less than $n$, where $n\ge1$. We first obtain the controlled partial homotopy required by Lemma <a href="#lem:controlled-additivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:controlled-additivity">6</a>. Begin with the map induced by $f$ and choose the vertex paths just described for its degree-zero homotopy to the constant map. Suppose more generally that a power of $f$ and its homotopy have been constructed through degree $q-1$, with $1\le q<n$. For every normalized $q$-simplex $c$, the missing boundary equation for its homotopy value has right side $$F(c)-H(\partial c),$$ where $F$ is the current chain map. For $q\ge1$ this is a cycle: apply the already established homotopy equation to $\partial c$. All these cycles, for all basis simplices $c$, have a common finite bound. The inductive assertion in degree $q$ supplies one further power of $f$ that sends all of them to boundaries at a common bound. Apply that power also to the previously constructed map and homotopy, then choose a finite filling for each of these boundary equations. Extending on the normalized basis defines $H_q$ with the required control. For $n=1$ only the initial vertex homotopy is needed.

After finitely many such steps, the map induced by some power $f^k$ has a controlled chain homotopy to the constant map through degrees less than $n$. The homotopy is not asserted to be $U$-equivariant. The same exponent works on all basis simplices because at each step the induction assertion applies to every cycle in one bounded complex.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:controlled-additivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:controlled-additivity">6</a> to every orthogonal relation in both finite idempotent systems, enlarging the output bound once to cover all of them. For a fixed $n$-cycle $z$, set $$z_E=[(h_Ef^k)_*z]$$ in the homology of this single bounded output complex. These classes satisfy all relations of Lemma <a href="#lem:idempotents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:idempotents">2</a>. The identity class is the same in both systems, namely $$z_I=[(f^{k+1})_*z].$$ It is annihilated by both $2$ and $3$, and hence is zero. The bound and exponent were chosen before $z$, so the conclusion is uniform over all cycles of the input bound. This completes the induction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Products of regular modules and the HNN sequence

We now complete the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:torus">3</a>. Put $J=T(U,f)$. Since $f$ is injective, the direct limit of the system $U\xrightarrow f U\xrightarrow f\cdots$ is an increasing union of copies of $U$. Its stage shift is an automorphism, and adjoining that automorphism gives $J$. Explicitly, its $i$th copy maps into $J$ by $u\mapsto t^iut^{-i}$; these maps give the inverse to the direct-limit description. In particular $U$ embeds in $J$, and every element of $J$ has the form $t^p u t^{-q}$ with $p,q\ge0$.

There is a $J$-tree with vertices the left cosets $jU$ and one edge indexed by $jU$ from $jU$ to $jtU$. The second endpoint is independent of the representative because $ut=tf(u)$. Every vertex has one outgoing edge, and its height increases by one. The normal form just given shows that every forward ray eventually meets the ray from $U$. Thus the graph is connected. An undirected cycle would have a vertex of least height with two outgoing edges, which is impossible. It is therefore a tree, with one vertex orbit and one edge orbit, both having stabilizer $U$.

<a id="eq:ascending-hnn-homology"></a>
<a id="eq:coefficient-shift"></a>
For any right $\mathbb ZJ$-module $M$, the tree gives the long exact homology sequence containing $$\label{eq:ascending-hnn-homology}
 \begin{split}
 H_n(U,M)&\xrightarrow{\,1-F\,}H_n(U,M)
       \longrightarrow H_n(J,M)\\
       &\longrightarrow H_{n-1}(U,M)
          \xrightarrow{\,1-F\,}H_{n-1}(U,M).
 \end{split}$$ We specify the chain map and a construction of this sequence. Induce the homogeneous resolution to obtain $P_* =\mathbb ZJ\otimes_{\mathbb ZU}C_*(U)$. On it, the second endpoint map is $$j\otimes c\longmapsto jt\otimes f_*(c).$$ It is well defined, again because $ut=tf(u)$. The mapping cone of the difference of the two endpoint maps resolves $\mathbb Z$: first taking homology of the induced resolutions recovers the augmented cellular chains of the tree, which are exact. Tensoring that cone with $M$ gives <a href="#eq:ascending-hnn-homology" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ascending-hnn-homology">[eq:ascending-hnn-homology]</a>. In the resulting complex $M\otimes_{\mathbb ZU}C_*(U)$, its map $F$ is $$\label{eq:coefficient-shift}
 F(m\otimes c)=mt\otimes f_*(c).$$

<a id="eq:bounded-product-chains"></a>
Take now $M=(\mathbb ZJ)^{\mathcal I}$ for an arbitrary set $\mathcal I$. We claim that $F$ is locally nilpotent on $H_i(U,M)$ for every $i>0$. To interpret chains, let $\mathcal S_q(A)$ be the finite set of normalized $q$-simplex types with bound $A$. The freeness of these types gives $$\label{eq:bounded-product-chains}
 \begin{split}
 M\otimes_{\mathbb ZU}C_q^A(U)
 &\cong\bigoplus_{s\in\mathcal S_q(A)} M\\
 &\cong\prod_{\alpha\in\mathcal I}
       \left(\bigoplus_{s\in\mathcal S_q(A)}\mathbb ZJ\right).
 \end{split}$$ The second isomorphism uses finiteness of $\mathcal S_q(A)$.

Every tensor chain involves finitely many simplex types, so lies in such a bounded complex. In each coordinate $\alpha$, expand its $\mathbb ZJ$ coefficients. The result is a finite ordinary chain in tuples contained in left cosets $jU$, with all within-coset differences in one bound $A$ independent of $\alpha$. Conversely, any family of such chains, finite in each coordinate and sharing one bound, defines a tensor chain by <a href="#eq:bounded-product-chains" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-product-chains">[eq:bounded-product-chains]</a>. The number of terms and the coefficient supports may vary without bound as $\alpha$ varies.
